People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

I don’t know that it’s all of my senses, exactly. Like, I’m not smelling it and hearing everything like I’m fully standing there again.

It’s more… I’ll be in the middle of something totally ordinary at work, like reviewing a policy or looking through a loss report, and then this really sharp image hits me — the worker on the floor, and the soot in the air. And it’s weird because I can have my spreadsheet open and my coffee right there, and part of me knows I’m at my desk, but my body reacts before I can stop it. Like I tense up before I’ve even had time to think, “Okay, this is just in my head.”

Yeah… kind of. Not like I fully think I’m back there, if that makes sense. It’s more that I’ll be doing something normal at work — reviewing a policy or looking at some loss report, you know, the kind with all the boring little tables — and then suddenly I get hit with this really sharp image of the worker on the floor, and the soot in the air.\n\nAnd my body just reacts before I can stop it. Like I haven’t even had time to think, “Okay, that’s just a memory,” and I’m already… tense, like I’m bracing. So, not exactly all the way back there, but it feels way too close.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're completely back at the warehouse inspection, with all of your senses, like you're really there again?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it’s all of my senses, exactly. Like, I’m not smelling it and hearing everything like I’m fully standing there again.\n\nIt’s more… I’ll be in the middle of something totally ordinary at work, like reviewing a policy or looking through a loss report, and then this really sharp image hits me — the worker on the floor, and the soot in the air. And it’s weird because I can have my spreadsheet open and my coffee right there, and part of me knows I’m at my desk, but my body reacts before I can stop it. Like I tense up before I’ve even had time to think, “Okay, this is just in my head.”\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're seeing or experiencing the warehouse inspection again, as if it's really happening to you right now?\n\nPatient: Yeah… kind of. Not like I fully think I’m back there, if that makes sense. It’s more like I’ll be doing something normal — reviewing a policy, or one of those loss reports with all the little columns, and I’ll be trying to focus, because if I don’t, I miss some tiny exclusion clause and then it becomes this whole thing later…\n\nBut then, out of nowhere, I get hit with this really sharp picture of him on the floor. And the soot in the air. It’s so fast, and my body reacts before I can stop it — like I tense up before I’ve even had time to remind myself I’m just sitting at my desk. So it doesn’t feel like it’s literally happening again, but in that moment it feels… way too real.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these flashbacks and sleep disturbances since the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: About a week, I think. It started pretty much right after that warehouse inspection — the one where the explosion happened.\n\nI keep thinking, if I’d just taken a different route through the building or if the inspection had been scheduled another day… I don’t know, that’s probably not what you asked. But yeah, since then. Around a week.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're losing touch with reality when these images of the warehouse inspection hit you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’m losing touch with reality exactly… I mean, I usually know I’m at my desk, or wherever I am. It’s just that during the day, I’ll be reviewing a policy or looking at a loss report, and then suddenly this sharp image hits me — the worker on the floor, the soot in the air — and my body reacts before I can stop it.\n\nLike I’ll tense up or freeze for a second before I’ve even really processed what’s happening. It’s strange, because part of me knows it’s not happening right then, but it still feels really immediate. And then I’ll try to go back to the report, but of course then I’m thinking about how I need to not make mistakes, because those reports are already so detail-heavy… sorry. No, I don’t think I’m completely out of reality. It just feels too real for a moment.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific people or places that you've been avoiding since the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly anything connected to that warehouse file. I know that sounds ridiculous because it’s literally part of my job, but I’ve been kind of… not opening it unless I absolutely have to.\n\nAnd calls about industrial accounts — I’ve been letting those go to voicemail. I tell myself I’ll call them back when I’m more prepared, but then I see the subject line or the account name and my stomach just drops. It’s weird, because normally I’m the person who’s on top of those things. I even color-code my follow-ups, which my coworker teases me about, but lately I just… avoid it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or responsible in any way for what happened during the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I keep going around in circles with it. Part of me knows I wasn’t in charge of the whole situation, but then I start thinking about whether I missed something, or if I should’ve noticed something earlier.\n\nI mean, with underwriting, you’re trained to look for risk everywhere — that’s literally the job — and sometimes I’ll remember totally unrelated files, like this apartment complex account where there were all these tiny notes buried in the inspection report, and I caught them because I was being obsessive about the details. I don’t know if “blame” is exactly the word… maybe sometimes, but then I talk myself out of it, and then I’m back in it again.

It’s more like I keep replaying the whole thing and wondering if I should’ve caught something sooner. Which is probably what I already said, sorry. I do that — I get stuck on a loop. So I guess I don’t fully know. Part of me says it wasn’t all on me, but part of me keeps asking what I missed.

It’s more that I’ll be doing something normal at work — reviewing a policy or looking at some loss report, you know, the kind with all the boring little tables — and then suddenly I get hit with this really sharp image of the worker on the floor, and the soot in the air.\n\nAnd my body just reacts before I can stop it. Like I haven’t even had time to think, “Okay, that’s just a memory,” and I’m already… tense, like I’m bracing. So, not exactly all the way back there, but it feels way too close.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're completely back at the warehouse inspection, with all of your senses, like you're really there again?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it’s all of my senses, exactly. Like, I’m not smelling it and hearing everything like I’m fully standing there again.\n\nIt’s more… I’ll be in the middle of something totally ordinary at work, like reviewing a policy or looking through a loss report, and then this really sharp image hits me — the worker on the floor, and the soot in the air. And it’s weird because I can have my spreadsheet open and my coffee right there, and part of me knows I’m at my desk, but my body reacts before I can stop it. Like I tense up before I’ve even had time to think, “Okay, this is just in my head.”\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're seeing or experiencing the warehouse inspection again, as if it's really happening to you right now?\n\nPatient: Yeah… kind of. Not like I fully think I’m back there, if that makes sense. It’s more like I’ll be doing something normal — reviewing a policy, or one of those loss reports with all the little columns, and I’ll be trying to focus, because if I don’t, I miss some tiny exclusion clause and then it becomes this whole thing later…\n\nBut then, out of nowhere, I get hit with this really sharp picture of him on the floor. And the soot in the air. It’s so fast, and my body reacts before I can stop it — like I tense up before I’ve even had time to remind myself I’m just sitting at my desk. So it doesn’t feel like it’s literally happening again, but in that moment it feels… way too real.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these flashbacks and sleep disturbances since the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: About a week, I think. It started pretty much right after that warehouse inspection — the one where the explosion happened.\n\nI keep thinking, if I’d just taken a different route through the building or if the inspection had been scheduled another day… I don’t know, that’s probably not what you asked. But yeah, since then. Around a week.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're losing touch with reality when these images of the warehouse inspection hit you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’m losing touch with reality exactly… I mean, I usually know I’m at my desk, or wherever I am. It’s just that during the day, I’ll be reviewing a policy or looking at a loss report, and then suddenly this sharp image hits me — the worker on the floor, the soot in the air — and my body reacts before I can stop it.\n\nLike I’ll tense up or freeze for a second before I’ve even really processed what’s happening. It’s strange, because part of me knows it’s not happening right then, but it still feels really immediate. And then I’ll try to go back to the report, but of course then I’m thinking about how I need to not make mistakes, because those reports are already so detail-heavy… sorry. No, I don’t think I’m completely out of reality. It just feels too real for a moment.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific people or places that you've been avoiding since the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly anything connected to that warehouse file. I know that sounds ridiculous because it’s literally part of my job, but I’ve been kind of… not opening it unless I absolutely have to.\n\nAnd calls about industrial accounts — I’ve been letting those go to voicemail. I tell myself I’ll call them back when I’m more prepared, but then I see the subject line or the account name and my stomach just drops. It’s weird, because normally I’m the person who’s on top of those things. I even color-code my follow-ups, which my coworker teases me about, but lately I just… avoid it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or responsible in any way for what happened during the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I keep going around in circles with it. Part of me knows I wasn’t in charge of the whole situation, but then I start thinking about whether I missed something, or if I should’ve noticed something earlier.\n\nI mean, with underwriting, you’re trained to look for risk everywhere — that’s literally the job — and sometimes I’ll remember totally unrelated files, like this apartment complex account where there were all these tiny notes buried in the inspection report, and I caught them because I was being obsessive about the details. So then I think, why didn’t I catch something here?\n\nSorry, I’m kind of rambling. I guess… I’m not sure if I feel responsible exactly. But I keep questioning myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you blame yourself for what happened during the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if “blame” is exactly the word… maybe sometimes, but then I talk myself out of it, and then I’m back in it again.\n\nIt’s more like I keep replaying the whole thing and wondering if I should’ve caught something sooner. Which is probably what I already said, sorry. I do that — I get stuck on a loop. Like at work, if there’s a discrepancy in a file, I can’t just leave it alone, even if it’s probably nothing. My manager used to say that was a strength, being thorough, but now it feels like… I don’t know, like if I was that thorough, then why didn’t I prevent it?\n\nSo I guess I don’t fully know. Part of me says it wasn’t all on me, but part of me keeps asking what I missed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I honestly don’t know. I keep trying to answer that in my head and it never really lands anywhere solid.

Like, part of me says no, because I wasn’t… I wasn’t the only person involved, and I didn’t control the whole warehouse or what was going on there. So I guess the real answer is I’m not sure. I can’t point to one specific thing and say, “If I’d done that, it wouldn’t have happened.” But I keep wondering if there was something.

But during the day I’ll be doing something totally normal, like reviewing a policy or a loss report, and then this image just slams into my head — the worker on the floor, the soot kind of hanging in the air.\n\nAnd my body reacts before I even… before I can talk myself out of it. Which is annoying, because I’ll be at my desk with my coffee and my highlighters and everything is normal, and then suddenly it’s like my chest knows before my brain catches up.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're completely back at the warehouse inspection, reliving it as if it's happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Completely back? Um… I don’t know if I’d say completely, like I’m standing there again. It’s more that I’ll be doing something normal at work — reviewing a policy or looking at some loss report, you know, the kind with all the boring little tables — and then suddenly I get hit with this really sharp image of the worker on the floor, and the soot in the air.\n\nAnd my body just reacts before I can stop it. Like I haven’t even had time to think, “Okay, that’s just a memory,” and I’m already… tense, like I’m bracing. So, not exactly all the way back there, but it feels way too close.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're completely back at the warehouse inspection, with all of your senses, like you're really there again?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it’s all of my senses, exactly. Like, I’m not smelling it and hearing everything like I’m fully standing there again.\n\nIt’s more… I’ll be in the middle of something totally ordinary at work, like reviewing a policy or looking through a loss report, and then this really sharp image hits me — the worker on the floor, and the soot in the air. And it’s weird because I can have my spreadsheet open and my coffee right there, and part of me knows I’m at my desk, but my body reacts before I can stop it. Like I tense up before I’ve even had time to think, “Okay, this is just in my head.”\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're seeing or experiencing the warehouse inspection again, as if it's really happening to you right now?\n\nPatient: Yeah… kind of. Not like I fully think I’m back there, if that makes sense. It’s more like I’ll be doing something normal — reviewing a policy, or one of those loss reports with all the little columns, and I’ll be trying to focus, because if I don’t, I miss some tiny exclusion clause and then it becomes this whole thing later…\n\nBut then, out of nowhere, I get hit with this really sharp picture of him on the floor. And the soot in the air. It’s so fast, and my body reacts before I can stop it — like I tense up before I’ve even had time to remind myself I’m just sitting at my desk. So it doesn’t feel like it’s literally happening again, but in that moment it feels… way too real.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these flashbacks and sleep disturbances since the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: About a week, I think. It started pretty much right after that warehouse inspection — the one where the explosion happened.\n\nI keep thinking, if I’d just taken a different route through the building or if the inspection had been scheduled another day… I don’t know, that’s probably not what you asked. But yeah, since then. Around a week.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're losing touch with reality when these images of the warehouse inspection hit you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’m losing touch with reality exactly… I mean, I usually know I’m at my desk, or wherever I am. It’s just that during the day, I’ll be reviewing a policy or looking at a loss report, and then suddenly this sharp image hits me — the worker on the floor, the soot in the air — and my body reacts before I can stop it.\n\nLike I’ll tense up or freeze for a second before I’ve even really processed what’s happening. It’s strange, because part of me knows it’s not happening right then, but it still feels really immediate. And then I’ll try to go back to the report, but of course then I’m thinking about how I need to not make mistakes, because those reports are already so detail-heavy… sorry. No, I don’t think I’m completely out of reality. It just feels too real for a moment.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific people or places that you've been avoiding since the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly anything connected to that warehouse file. I know that sounds ridiculous because it’s literally part of my job, but I’ve been kind of… not opening it unless I absolutely have to.\n\nAnd calls about industrial accounts — I’ve been letting those go to voicemail. I tell myself I’ll call them back when I’m more prepared, but then I see the subject line or the account name and my stomach just drops. It’s weird, because normally I’m the person who’s on top of those things. I even color-code my follow-ups, which my coworker teases me about, but lately I just… avoid it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or responsible in any way for what happened during the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I keep going around in circles with it. Part of me knows I wasn’t in charge of the whole situation, but then I start thinking about whether I missed something, or if I should’ve noticed something earlier.\n\nI mean, with underwriting, you’re trained to look for risk everywhere — that’s literally the job — and sometimes I’ll remember totally unrelated files, like this apartment complex account where there were all these tiny notes buried in the inspection report, and I caught them because I was being obsessive about the details. So then I think, why didn’t I catch something here?\n\nSorry, I’m kind of rambling. I guess… I’m not sure if I feel responsible exactly. But I keep questioning myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you blame yourself for what happened during the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if “blame” is exactly the word… maybe sometimes, but then I talk myself out of it, and then I’m back in it again.\n\nIt’s more like I keep replaying the whole thing and wondering if I should’ve caught something sooner. Which is probably what I already said, sorry. I do that — I get stuck on a loop. Like at work, if there’s a discrepancy in a file, I can’t just leave it alone, even if it’s probably nothing. My manager used to say that was a strength, being thorough, but now it feels like… I don’t know, like if I was that thorough, then why didn’t I prevent it?\n\nSo I guess I don’t fully know. Part of me says it wasn’t all on me, but part of me keeps asking what I missed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you could have done something to prevent the explosion at the warehouse?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know. I keep trying to answer that in my head and it never really lands anywhere solid.\n\nLike, part of me says no, because I wasn’t… I wasn’t the only person involved, and I didn’t control the whole warehouse or what was going on there. But then another part of me goes, okay, but if I had pushed harder on some detail, or asked one more question, or flagged something differently—maybe something would’ve changed.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about other inspections, other files, even ones that have nothing to do with this. Like there was this manufacturing account last year where I noticed one weird line in a report and it turned into this whole back-and-forth with the broker, and everyone acted like I was being overly cautious at first, but it mattered. So now my brain keeps comparing it, like… why didn’t I have that feeling this time?\n\nSo I guess the real answer is I’m not sure. I can’t point to one specific thing and say, “If I’d done that, it wouldn’t have happened.” But I keep wondering if there was something.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other things, besides work-related tasks, that you've been avoiding because they remind you of the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s still tied to work stuff, I think. I mean… I know you asked besides work-related tasks, but that’s where it keeps showing up.\n\nI’ve been avoiding the warehouse file itself. Like it’s just sitting there, and even seeing the subject line or opening one of those emails makes my stomach drop. And calls about industrial accounts — I’ve let them go to voicemail, which isn’t really like me. Usually I’m the person who answers because I hate things piling up.\n\nSorry, I know that’s still work-related. I’m trying to think of other things, but that’s the clearest one.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy since the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t think I’ve noticed it in a clear way, like “I used to love this and now I don’t.”\n\nI haven’t really been tracking it, honestly. I still kind of… intend to do normal things, if that makes sense. Like I’ll think about watching something or making plans, and then I get distracted by something else, like emails or laundry or—sorry, that’s probably not what you asked.\n\nI guess I can’t say for sure that I’ve lost interest. I just haven’t really paid attention to whether I’m enjoying things the same way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I mean… I know you asked besides work-related tasks, but that’s where it keeps showing up.\n\nI’ve been avoiding the warehouse file itself. Like it’s just sitting there, and even seeing the subject line or opening one of those emails makes my stomach drop. And calls about industrial accounts — I’ve let them go to voicemail, which isn’t really like me. Usually I’m the person who answers because I hate things piling up.\n\nSorry, I know that’s still work-related. I’m trying to think of other things, but that’s the clearest one.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy since the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t think I’ve noticed it in a clear way, like “I used to love this and now I don’t.”\n\nI haven’t really been tracking it, honestly. I still kind of… intend to do normal things, if that makes sense. Like I’ll think about watching something or making plans, and then I get distracted by something else, like emails or laundry or—sorry, that’s probably not what you asked.\n\nI guess I can’t say for sure that I’ve lost interest. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… daily life, definitely, mostly work. Relationships, I don’t know, I haven’t really thought about that part as much.

But at work it’s starting to show. I’ve missed deadlines on a few underwriting reviews, which is… not like me, or at least not how I want to be seen. And I’ve started handing off some of the industrial account calls I’m supposed to be managing.

It’s frustrating because I just took on this role, and I was trying so hard to prove I could handle it. Like when I first got promoted, I bought this new planner and had all these little systems—color coding, reminders, all of that—and now it feels stupid because the systems are still there, I’m just not doing the things.

So yes, I think it’s making me look unreliable. That’s probably the part that scares me most, because I don’t want people thinking they made a mistake trusting me with the accounts.

I don’t know if guilt is the right word. I keep trying to sort of… replay whether I should’ve handled myself better, but then I get stuck on little details that probably don’t matter, like where I was standing or what I’d already written down.

It’s strange, because part of me knows it’s not happening right then, but it still feels really immediate. So it doesn’t feel like it’s literally happening again, but in that moment it feels… way too real.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these flashbacks and sleep disturbances since the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: About a week, I think. It started pretty much right after that warehouse inspection — the one where the explosion happened.\n\nI keep thinking, if I’d just taken a different route through the building or if the inspection had been scheduled another day… I don’t know, that’s probably not what you asked. But yeah, since then. Around a week.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're losing touch with reality when these images of the warehouse inspection hit you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’m losing touch with reality exactly… I mean, I usually know I’m at my desk, or wherever I am. It’s just that during the day, I’ll be reviewing a policy or looking at a loss report, and then suddenly this sharp image hits me — the worker on the floor, the soot in the air — and my body reacts before I can stop it.\n\nLike I’ll tense up or freeze for a second before I’ve even really processed what’s happening. It’s strange, because part of me knows it’s not happening right then, but it still feels really immediate. And then I’ll try to go back to the report, but of course then I’m thinking about how I need to not make mistakes, because those reports are already so detail-heavy… sorry. No, I don’t think I’m completely out of reality. It just feels too real for a moment.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific people or places that you've been avoiding since the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly anything connected to that warehouse file. I know that sounds ridiculous because it’s literally part of my job, but I’ve been kind of… not opening it unless I absolutely have to.\n\nAnd calls about industrial accounts — I’ve been letting those go to voicemail. I tell myself I’ll call them back when I’m more prepared, but then I see the subject line or the account name and my stomach just drops. It’s weird, because normally I’m the person who’s on top of those things. I even color-code my follow-ups, which my coworker teases me about, but lately I just… avoid it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or responsible in any way for what happened during the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I keep going around in circles with it. Part of me knows I wasn’t in charge of the whole situation, but then I start thinking about whether I missed something, or if I should’ve noticed something earlier.\n\nI mean, with underwriting, you’re trained to look for risk everywhere — that’s literally the job — and sometimes I’ll remember totally unrelated files, like this apartment complex account where there were all these tiny notes buried in the inspection report, and I caught them because I was being obsessive about the details. So then I think, why didn’t I catch something here?\n\nSorry, I’m kind of rambling. I guess… I’m not sure if I feel responsible exactly. But I keep questioning myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you blame yourself for what happened during the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if “blame” is exactly the word… maybe sometimes, but then I talk myself out of it, and then I’m back in it again.\n\nIt’s more like I keep replaying the whole thing and wondering if I should’ve caught something sooner. Which is probably what I already said, sorry. I do that — I get stuck on a loop. Like at work, if there’s a discrepancy in a file, I can’t just leave it alone, even if it’s probably nothing. My manager used to say that was a strength, being thorough, but now it feels like… I don’t know, like if I was that thorough, then why didn’t I prevent it?\n\nSo I guess I don’t fully know. Part of me says it wasn’t all on me, but part of me keeps asking what I missed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you could have done something to prevent the explosion at the warehouse?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know. I keep trying to answer that in my head and it never really lands anywhere solid.\n\nLike, part of me says no, because I wasn’t… I wasn’t the only person involved, and I didn’t control the whole warehouse or what was going on there. But then another part of me goes, okay, but if I had pushed harder on some detail, or asked one more question, or flagged something differently—maybe something would’ve changed.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about other inspections, other files, even ones that have nothing to do with this. Like there was this manufacturing account last year where I noticed one weird line in a report and it turned into this whole back-and-forth with the broker, and everyone acted like I was being overly cautious at first, but it mattered. So now my brain keeps comparing it, like… why didn’t I have that feeling this time?\n\nSo I guess the real answer is I’m not sure. I can’t point to one specific thing and say, “If I’d done that, it wouldn’t have happened.” But I keep wondering if there was something.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other things, besides work-related tasks, that you've been avoiding because they remind you of the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s still tied to work stuff, I think. I just haven’t really paid attention to whether I’m enjoying things the same way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems with your relationships or daily life because of what's been happening since the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: Yeah… daily life, definitely, mostly work. Relationships, I don’t know, I haven’t really thought about that part as much.\n\nBut at work it’s starting to show. I’ve missed deadlines on a few underwriting reviews, which is… not like me, or at least not how I want to be seen. And I’ve started handing off some of the industrial account calls I’m supposed to be managing.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I just took on this role, and I was trying so hard to prove I could handle it. Like when I first got promoted, I bought this new planner and had all these little systems—color coding, reminders, all of that—and now it feels stupid because the systems are still there, I’m just not doing the things.\n\nSo yes, I think it’s making me look unreliable. That’s probably the part that scares me most, because I don’t want people thinking they made a mistake trusting me with the accounts.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed about what happened during the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if guilt is the right word. I keep trying to sort of… replay whether I should’ve handled myself better, but then I get stuck on little details that probably don’t matter, like where I was standing or what I’d already written down.\n\nAshamed, maybe? I hate saying that because it sounds dramatic, and I’m usually pretty good at keeping things contained. My mom always used to say I could make a checklist for anything, which is funny because that’s basically my whole job now, except—sorry.\n\nI guess I’m not sure. My manager used to say that was a strength, being thorough, but now it feels like… I don’t know, like if I was that thorough, then why didn’t I prevent it?\n\nSo I guess I don’t fully know. Part of me says it wasn’t all on me, but part of me keeps asking what I missed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you could have done something to prevent the explosion at the warehouse?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know. I keep trying to answer that in my head and it never really lands anywhere solid.\n\nLike, part of me says no, because I wasn’t… I wasn’t the only person involved, and I didn’t control the whole warehouse or what was going on there. But then another part of me goes, okay, but if I had pushed harder on some detail, or asked one more question, or flagged something differently—maybe something would’ve changed.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about other inspections, other files, even ones that have nothing to do with this. Like there was this manufacturing account last year where I noticed one weird line in a report and it turned into this whole back-and-forth with the broker, and everyone acted like I was being overly cautious at first, but it mattered. So now my brain keeps comparing it, like… why didn’t I have that feeling this time?\n\nSo I guess the real answer is I’m not sure. I mean, I’m not like refusing to go outside or anything, but with work stuff, yes.\n\nThe warehouse file is the big one. I keep putting it off, even though it’s sitting there and I know I need to deal with it. And when emails come in about it, or about industrial accounts, I’ll see the subject line and my stomach just drops. Then I’ll suddenly decide I should answer some totally different email first, or reorganize something, which is ridiculous because that doesn’t actually help.\n\nI’ve also let calls about those industrial accounts go to voicemail. I tell myself I’m just “batching calls” or whatever, because that sounds more professional, but honestly I think I just don’t want to hear anything connected to that inspection.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you're a bad person because of what happened during the warehouse inspection?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 27 / insurance_underwriter / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
A bad person? I… no, I don’t think I’d put it like that.

I mean, I’ve wondered if I looked incompetent, which is different, I guess. Like when you send an email and then reread it five times because the tone feels wrong—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. I don’t think I feel morally bad, like I did something cruel or intentional.

It’s more… I don’t like how I handled things after, maybe. But “bad person” feels too big, or not quite the right category.

I keep avoiding the warehouse file at work. I know that sounds like dodging it, because it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're reliving the warehouse inspection, like it's happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not like I’m literally back there all the way, I don’t think. But during the day I’ll be doing something totally normal, like reviewing a policy or a loss report, and then this image just slams into my head — the worker on the floor, the soot kind of hanging in the air.\n\nAnd my body reacts before I even… before I can talk myself out of it. Which is annoying, because I’ll be at my desk with my coffee and my highlighters and everything is normal, and then suddenly it’s like my chest knows before my brain catches up.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're completely back at the warehouse inspection, reliving it as if it's happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Completely back? Um… I don’t know if I’d say completely, like I’m standing there again. It’s more that I’ll be doing something normal at work — reviewing a policy or looking at some loss report, you know, the kind with all the boring little tables — and then suddenly I get hit with this really sharp image of the worker on the floor, and the soot in the air.\n\nAnd my body just reacts before I can stop it. Like I haven’t even had time to think, “Okay, that’s just a memory,” and I’m already… tense, like I’m bracing. So, not exactly all the way back there, but it feels way too close.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're completely back at the warehouse inspection, with all of your senses, like you're really there again?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it’s all of my senses, exactly. Like, I’m not smelling it and hearing everything like I’m fully standing there again.\n\nIt’s more… I’ll be in the middle of something totally ordinary at work, like reviewing a policy or looking through a loss report, and then this really sharp image hits me — the worker on the floor, and the soot in the air. And it’s weird because I can have my spreadsheet open and my coffee right there, and part of me knows I’m at my desk, but my body reacts before I can stop it. Like I tense up before I’ve even had time to think, “Okay, this is just in my head.”\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're seeing or experiencing the warehouse inspection again, as if it's really happening to you right now?\n\nPatient: Yeah… kind of. Not like I fully think I’m back there, if that makes sense. It’s more like I’ll be doing something normal — reviewing a policy, or one of those loss reports with all the little columns, and I’ll be trying to focus, because if I don’t, I miss some tiny exclusion clause and then it becomes this whole thing later…\n\nBut then, out of nowhere, I get hit with this really sharp picture of him on the floor. And the soot in the air. It’s so fast, and my body reacts before I can stop it — like I tense up before I’ve even had time to remind myself I’m just sitting at my desk. So it doesn’t feel like it’s literally happening again, but in that moment it feels… way too real.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these flashbacks and sleep disturbances since the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: About a week, I think. Around a week.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're losing touch with reality when these images of the warehouse inspection hit you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’m losing touch with reality exactly… I mean, I usually know I’m at my desk, or wherever I am. It’s just that during the day, I’ll be reviewing a policy or looking at a loss report, and then suddenly this sharp image hits me — the worker on the floor, the soot in the air — and my body reacts before I can stop it.\n\nLike I’ll tense up or freeze for a second before I’ve even really processed what’s happening. It’s strange, because part of me knows it’s not happening right then, but it still feels really immediate. And then I’ll try to go back to the report, but of course then I’m thinking about how I need to not make mistakes, because those reports are already so detail-heavy… sorry. No, I don’t think I’m completely out of reality. It just feels too real for a moment.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific people or places that you've been avoiding since the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly anything connected to that warehouse file. I know that sounds ridiculous because it’s literally part of my job, but I’ve been kind of… not opening it unless I absolutely have to.\n\nAnd calls about industrial accounts — I’ve been letting those go to voicemail. I tell myself I’ll call them back when I’m more prepared, but then I see the subject line or the account name and my stomach just drops. It’s weird, because normally I’m the person who’s on top of those things. I even color-code my follow-ups, which my coworker teases me about, but lately I just… avoid it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or responsible in any way for what happened during the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I keep going around in circles with it. Part of me knows I wasn’t in charge of the whole situation, but then I start thinking about whether I missed something, or if I should’ve noticed something earlier.\n\nI mean, with underwriting, you’re trained to look for risk everywhere — that’s literally the job — and sometimes I’ll remember totally unrelated files, like this apartment complex account where there were all these tiny notes buried in the inspection report, and I caught them because I was being obsessive about the details. So then I think, why didn’t I catch something here?\n\nSorry, I’m kind of rambling. I guess… I’m not sure if I feel responsible exactly. But I keep questioning myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you blame yourself for what happened during the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if “blame” is exactly the word… maybe sometimes, but then I talk myself out of it, and then I’m back in it again.\n\nIt’s more like I keep replaying the whole thing and wondering if I should’ve caught something sooner. Which is probably what I already said, sorry. I do that — I get stuck on a loop. Like at work, if there’s a discrepancy in a file, I can’t just leave it alone, even if it’s probably nothing. My manager used to say that was a strength, being thorough, but now it feels like… I don’t know, like if I was that thorough, then why didn’t I prevent it?\n\nSo I guess I don’t fully know. Part of me says it wasn’t all on me, but part of me keeps asking what I missed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you could have done something to prevent the explosion at the warehouse?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know. I keep trying to answer that in my head and it never really lands anywhere solid.\n\nLike, part of me says no, because I wasn’t… I wasn’t the only person involved, and I didn’t control the whole warehouse or what was going on there. But then another part of me goes, okay, but if I had pushed harder on some detail, or asked one more question, or flagged something differently—maybe something would’ve changed.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about other inspections, other files, even ones that have nothing to do with this. Like there was this manufacturing account last year where I noticed one weird line in a report and it turned into this whole back-and-forth with the broker, and everyone acted like I was being overly cautious at first, but it mattered. So now my brain keeps comparing it, like… why didn’t I have that feeling this time?\n\nSo I guess the real answer is I’m not sure. I can’t point to one specific thing and say, “If I’d done that, it wouldn’t have happened.” But I keep wondering if there was something.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other things, besides work-related tasks, that you've been avoiding because they remind you of the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s still tied to work stuff, I think. I mean… I know you asked besides work-related tasks, but that’s where it keeps showing up.\n\nI’ve been avoiding the warehouse file itself. Like it’s just sitting there, and even seeing the subject line or opening one of those emails makes my stomach drop. And calls about industrial accounts — I’ve let them go to voicemail, which isn’t really like me. Usually I’m the person who answers because I hate things piling up.\n\nSorry, I know that’s still work-related. I’m trying to think of other things, but that’s the clearest one.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy since the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t think I’ve noticed it in a clear way, like “I used to love this and now I don’t.”\n\nI haven’t really been tracking it, honestly. I still kind of… intend to do normal things, if that makes sense. Like I’ll think about watching something or making plans, and then I get distracted by something else, like emails or laundry or—sorry, that’s probably not what you asked.\n\nI guess I can’t say for sure that I’ve lost interest. I just haven’t really paid attention to whether I’m enjoying things the same way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems with your relationships or daily life because of what's been happening since the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: Yeah… daily life, definitely, mostly work. Like when I first got promoted, I bought this new planner and had all these little systems—color coding, reminders, all of that—and now it feels stupid because the systems are still there, I’m just not doing the things.\n\nSo yes, I think it’s making me look unreliable. That’s probably the part that scares me most, because I don’t want people thinking they made a mistake trusting me with the accounts.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed about what happened during the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if guilt is the right word. I keep trying to sort of… replay whether I should’ve handled myself better, but then I get stuck on little details that probably don’t matter, like where I was standing or what I’d already written down.\n\nAshamed, maybe? Or embarrassed. But I don’t know if that’s about what happened or about how I’ve been acting since. I hate saying that because it sounds dramatic, and I’m usually pretty good at keeping things contained. My mom always used to say I could make a checklist for anything, which is funny because that’s basically my whole job now, except—sorry.\n\nI guess I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say I have one clear feeling like guilt. It’s more just uncomfortable to think about.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding people, places, or activities that remind you of the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’m not like refusing to go outside or anything, but with work stuff, yes.\n\nThe warehouse file is the big one. Like I’ll think about watching something or making plans, and then I get distracted by something else, like emails or laundry or—sorry, that’s probably not what you asked.\n\nI guess I can’t say for sure that I’ve lost interest. I just haven’t really paid attention to whether I’m enjoying things the same way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems with your relationships or daily life because of what's been happening since the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: Yeah… daily life, definitely, mostly work. Relationships, I don’t know, I haven’t really thought about that part as much.\n\nBut at work it’s starting to show. I’ve missed deadlines on a few underwriting reviews, which is… not like me, or at least not how I want to be seen. And I’ve started handing off some of the industrial account calls I’m supposed to be managing.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I just took on this role, and I was trying so hard to prove I could handle it. Like when I first got promoted, I bought this new planner and had all these little systems—color coding, reminders, all of that—and now it feels stupid because the systems are still there, I’m just not doing the things.\n\nSo yes, I think it’s making me look unreliable. That’s probably the part that scares me most, because I don’t want people thinking they made a mistake trusting me with the accounts.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed about what happened during the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if guilt is the right word. I keep trying to sort of… replay whether I should’ve handled myself better, but then I get stuck on little details that probably don’t matter, like where I was standing or what I’d already written down.\n\nAshamed, maybe? Or embarrassed. But I don’t know if that’s about what happened or about how I’ve been acting since. I hate saying that because it sounds dramatic, and I’m usually pretty good at keeping things contained. My mom always used to say I could make a checklist for anything, which is funny because that’s basically my whole job now, except—sorry.\n\nI guess I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say I have one clear feeling like guilt. It’s more just uncomfortable to think about.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding people, places, or activities that remind you of the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’m not like refusing to go outside or anything, but with work stuff, yes.\n\nThe warehouse file is the big one. I keep putting it off, even though it’s sitting there and I know I need to deal with it. And when emails come in about it, or about industrial accounts, I’ll see the subject line and my stomach just drops. Then I’ll suddenly decide I should answer some totally different email first, or reorganize something, which is ridiculous because that doesn’t actually help.\n\nI’ve also let calls about those industrial accounts go to voicemail. I tell myself I’m just “batching calls” or whatever, because that sounds more professional, but honestly I think I just don’t want to hear anything connected to that inspection.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're a bad person because of what happened during the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: A bad person? I… no, I don’t think I’d put it like that.\n\nI mean, I’ve wondered if I looked incompetent, which is different, I guess. Like when you send an email and then reread it five times because the tone feels wrong—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. I don’t think I feel morally bad, like I did something cruel or intentional.\n\nIt’s more… I don’t like how I handled things after, maybe. But “bad person” feels too big, or not quite the right category."
